- [x] **The one-ply Runner ran into rezzed ICE it could not break — DONE** (`fix/heuristic-runner-run-term`, September 2026). With the evaluator's run term (+0.6 while a run is in progress) beating a credit click (+0.4) on every click, a heuristic Runner ran into rezzed ICE it could not break rather than building the credits for a breaker: 0.7 programs installed per game and ~100 subroutines broken against ~1,100 fired across 96 heuristic-vs-heuristic games, even with the strength-shortfall term making a pump worth its credits. The fix is the one this entry named: **`ACTIVE_RUN_WEIGHT` is now credited only when the Runner can afford to break every rezzed ICE still ahead of it** (`eval::run_is_breakable`), the cost read off the rig's own paid abilities — pump credits to close the strength shortfall, then break credits per pending subroutine, cheapest rig card wins. Three decisions, each recorded on the constant: the gate is *affordability*, not owning a matching breaker (Cleaver at 0 credits breaks nothing); only *rezzed* ICE counts, because an unrezzed card's identity in the determinized sample is a guess the real Runner cannot see and the Corp may never rez; ICE behind `run.position` is ignored, so the term also drops to zero mid-run when the Corp rezzes something unbreakable and jacking out becomes the preferred exit. `MctsAgent`, the uniform PUCT evaluator and the gym's shaped reward all inherit it (the retrain item below already stands).
  - **Measured (96 games, seed 1). Heuristic-vs-heuristic:** `SubroutineFired` 1,006 → 493 and `SubroutineBroken` 86 → 124; `RunEndedByEffect` 460 → 185 and `IceEncountered` 727 → 387 (the Runner stopped volunteering for encounters it could not pay for) while `RunInitiated` rose 1,810 → 2,509 and `RunSucceeded` 1,312 → 2,316 — the runs moved to servers it can get into; flatlines 58 → 31, Runner point wins 22 → 40, Corp point wins 16 → 25. Random-Corp-vs-heuristic-Runner: `SubroutineFired` 802 → 224, `RunEndedByEffect` 457 → 128, `JackOut` 3 → 10, flatlines 39 → 19, Runner wins 57 → 77. Heuristic-vs-random and random-vs-random are byte-identical before and after, as they must be (only the Runner evaluator changed).
- [x] **A savings term, and what it revealed — DONE, inert** (`fix/heuristic-runner-savings-term`, September 2026). `ProgramInstalled` was 66 in 96 heuristic-vs-heuristic games before and after the run-term fix: the conditional term removed the *bad* run, but its replacement is a *good* run (some server is always unprotected or unrezzed, worth +0.6) rather than a credit click (+0.4). The evaluator now charges `SAVINGS_SHORTFALL_WEIGHT` (0.3) per credit the Runner is short of the cheapest grip breaker that covers a subtype the rig cannot and fits in free memory (`eval::breaker_savings_shortfall`). It is a penalty on the *shortfall*, not a bonus on credits held, and that is the design: a bonus would vanish on install and fight the install it exists to enable (Cleaver's +1.3 would lose 3 × the weight); the shortfall is zero once the card is affordable, so a click that closes the gap is +0.7 against an open-server run's +0.6 and installing keeps its full margin. Tests pin all of that, and the agent test shows the Runner clicking for a credit over an open run with Cleaver in grip at 1 credit.
  - **Measured (96 games, seed 1): nothing moved.** Heuristic-vs-heuristic `ProgramInstalled` 66 → 64, Runner `GainCreditClick` 38 → 53, `RunInitiated` 2,509 → 2,422; random-Corp-vs-heuristic-Runner `ProgramInstalled` unchanged at 66, Runner `GainCreditClick` 15 → 29. Seatings without a heuristic Runner are byte-identical. **The reason is upstream of credits:** the heuristic Runner clicked `DrawCardClick` **once** in 96 games (the Corp, with its mandatory draw, never), because a card in grip is worth nothing to the evaluator. Its grip is the opening hand plus what Diesel and friends draw, so a breaker in the opening hand was already installed on turn one (5 starting credits cover Cleaver or Unity) and there is almost never an *unaffordable* breaker in hand to save for. Program installs are draw-limited, not credit-limited.
- [x] **The one-ply Runner never drew — DONE** (`fix/heuristic-runner-draw-term`, September 2026). `DrawCardClick` was 1 in 96 heuristic-vs-heuristic games (random-vs-random: 667) because a card in hand was worth nothing to the evaluator. The evaluator now charges `GRIP_SHORTFALL_WEIGHT` (0.7) per card the grip is below `GRIP_FLOOR` (3) — a shortfall below a floor, not a value per card, so a healthy hand's installs are not taxed: below the floor a draw is +0.7 against an open run's +0.6, installing from a 3-card grip loses 0.7 of Cleaver's +1.3 so the Runner draws one card first and then installs at full margin, and at the floor the term goes flat and runs resume. The floor sits under the hand limit, so a click-draw is never discarded. Runner-only: the Corp's mandatory draw hides the same blindness, and what a heuristic Corp should hold is a different question.
  - **Measured (96 games, seed 1). Heuristic-vs-heuristic:** `DrawCardClick` 1 → 388, `ProgramInstalled` 64 → 90 (0.94 per game — the run-term entry's 0.7 was the ceiling of the opening hand), `PlayEvent` 40 → 79, `SubroutineBroken` 125 → 196 with `IceEncountered` 389 → 555 (the Runner now meets ICE with a breaker), flatlines 32 → 6, Corp point wins 25 → 43 and Runner point wins 39 → 47 — games end on points rather than a hand-empty Runner dying to net damage; steps 42,306 → 56,796. Random-Corp-vs-heuristic-Runner: `DrawCardClick` 0 → 245, `ProgramInstalled` 66 → 81, flatlines 22 → 8, and the first three Runner deck-outs in a 96-game sample. Hardware and resource installs fell (29 → 19, 62 → 39): a thin grip now draws before it installs a cheap card. Seatings without a heuristic Runner are byte-identical. The savings term above is live as a consequence, since a drawn breaker can now be unaffordable.
- [x] **The heuristic Corp never drew and never rezzed a 2-cost asset — DONE** (`fix/heuristic-corp-draw-and-rez`, September 2026). Corp `DrawCardClick` was 0 in every heuristic seating: with only the mandatory draw feeding HQ the Corp deployed about one card a turn and spent the rest on credits (1.18 credit clicks per turn). Re-reading the same evaluator found a second blindness: a rezzed non-ICE card was worth `BOARD_PRESENCE_WEIGHT − UNREZZED_INSTALL_WEIGHT` = +0.4 minus its rez cost × 0.4, so nothing costing 2 or more was ever rezzed — Nico Campaign and Manegarm Skunkworks were installed 33 and 34 times in 96 heuristic-vs-heuristic games and rezzed never. Three weights moved together, each with its arithmetic on the constant: **`UNREZZED_INSTALL_WEIGHT` 0.6 → 1.0 and `REZZED_ICE_WEIGHT` 1.0 → 1.4** (the ICE rez delta, 1.4 − 0.4 × cost, is unchanged; a second ICE on a server is now +0.6 and happens, where it was +0.2 and never did); a new **`REZZED_ASSET_WEIGHT` 1.0** (rezzing a 2-cost asset is +0.2, and anything above zero is rezzed eventually because a rez costs no click and competes with `EndTurn` at 0); and an **HQ floor** mirroring the Runner's grip term (`HQ_SHORTFALL_WEIGHT` 0.5 below `HQ_FLOOR` 3, off while R&D is under `RD_DRAW_RESERVE` 5 so the Corp cannot draw itself into a deck-out). The install-weight bump is what makes the floor work: below it a draw must beat a credit (weight > 0.4), and installing *from* the floor then loses that same weight, so over the old 0.6 install the Corp would have stalled at the floor clicking for credits — the rhythm it already had.
  - **The floor found a latent stall, fixed in the same branch.** At floor 3, heuristic Corp vs random Runner, seed 77 (Discretion Advised vs Planning Ahead) ran to the 10,000-step budget: a parked card selection over an at-floor HQ, where confirming hands over a card (−0.5) and resolving the decision gains exactly `UNRESOLVED_DECISION_WEIGHT` (+0.5), so confirm tied with a toggle and the tie-break jitter random-walked `ToggleCardSelection` for 9,000 steps. The same cancellation was one card away for the Runner's 0.7 grip term. `UNRESOLVED_DECISION_WEIGHT` is 2.0 now, above every per-card term on either side, with a test pinning both sides.
  - **Measured (96 games, seed 1). Heuristic-vs-heuristic:** Corp `DrawCardClick` 0 → 138 (0.11 per turn), `InstallCard` per turn 0.92 → 1.06, credit clicks per turn 1.18 → 0.92, `IceRezzed` 941 → 986, Nico Campaign rezzed 0 → 35 of 37, Manegarm 0 → 33 of 33. The stronger board shows on the Runner's side of the same games: `IceEncountered` 555 → 445, `SubroutineBroken` 196 → 100, `RunSucceeded` 3,157 → 2,736. Outcomes are mixed — Corp point wins 43 → 37, Runner 47 → 53, `AgendaStolen` 218 → 227 — a Corp holding three cards in HQ gives HQ runs more to find, and the one-ply Corp still installs agendas wherever it can. Heuristic-Corp-vs-random-Runner: Corp draws 0 → 116, Corp wins 89 → 91 of 96. Random-vs-random is byte-identical; random-Corp-vs-heuristic-Runner differs only through the shared decision weight (169 keys, no metric moved by more than a few percent).
- [x] **Carmen, and where the Corp puts its agendas — DONE** (`fix/heuristic-carmen-and-agenda-placement`, September 2026). Carmen (5[c]) installed at −2.0 − 0.5 + 1.0 + 2.0 = +0.5, under an open run's +0.6, and from an at-floor grip (−0.7) at −0.2: never installed in any heuristic seating. **`BREAKER_COVERAGE_WEIGHT` 2.0 → 3.0** puts her at +1.5 (+0.8 from the floor) and every breaker in the pool above a run from anywhere. Every install was worth the same flat `UNREZZED_INSTALL_WEIGHT` wherever it went, so the one-ply Corp put an agenda in a fresh naked remote as readily as behind ICE and never preferred to ICE the remote it was scoring out of. **`AGENDA_PROTECTION_WEIGHT` 0.5 per ICE, capped at `AGENDA_PROTECTION_CAP` 2, on each installed agenda's server** (`eval::protected_agenda_ice`) — read on the agenda, not the ICE, so one term does both jobs: an agenda into a one-ICE remote is +0.5 over a naked one, and ICE in front of an installed agenda is +0.5 on top of the install, enough for a second ICE there (1[c]) to beat one elsewhere. The agent test seats both a protected and a naked remote and asserts the agenda goes behind the ICE.
  - **Measured (96 games, seed 1). Heuristic-vs-heuristic:** Carmen installed 0 → 22, `ProgramInstalled` 82 → 122, `SubroutineBroken` 100 → 199 with `SubroutineFired` 588 → 519 and `JackOut` 0 → 7; remote runs 1,639 → 1,300 (fewer naked remotes to poke) with `AgendaStolen` 227 → 200 and `AgendaScored` 176 → 208; Corp point wins 37 → 50, Runner 53 → 41. Random-Corp-vs-heuristic-Runner: `ProgramInstalled` 81 → 102. Heuristic-Corp-vs-random-Runner: unchanged within noise (Corp wins 91 → 91). Random-vs-random is byte-identical.
- [x] **`determinize`'s hidden-card pools were unordered, so every determinizing bot was nondeterministic run to run — DONE** (`fix/determinize-pool-order`, September 2026). The pools were built from `CardRegistry::iter`, which is `HashMap::values()` — a different order in every process — and `Pool::new`'s seeded shuffle only reproduces over an identical input, so the same seed sampled different hidden cards on each run. Re-confirmed before fixing: two runs of heuristic-vs-heuristic (96 games, seed 1) on unchanged code differed in **282 keys** (the 262 recorded when this was found was another pair of runs — the count itself is a random variable). `build_pools` now sorts the candidates by `CardId` before the shuffle (`CardId` derives `Ord` for it); the registry's own iteration order stays unspecified, since the requirement lives in the determinizer. **After: two runs are byte-identical**, and random-vs-random is byte-identical to the previous report (`trk-random-random.json`), as it must be with no determinizing bot seated. Heuristic seatings are therefore a valid byte-identical before/after measurement from this branch on; every "heuristic play shifted" note earlier in this file remains unproven and is left as written. Pinned by `the_same_seed_samples_the_same_hidden_cards_whatever_the_registry_order`, which builds the same registry in ascending and descending insertion order.
- [x] **`determinize` sampled identities as hidden cards — DONE** (same branch). Its pools were every registry card of a side, so a one-ply Runner could imagine *The Syndicate* in HQ or on top of R&D — a card the real game cannot hold there. `CardType::Identity` is filtered out of both pools, pinned by `identities_are_never_sampled_into_a_hidden_zone` (64 seeds over a registry carrying an identity per side; fails on the commit before it). **Measured as a bot change, and the first heuristic measurement in this file that is not noise** — the sorted-pool report against itself is the baseline. Heuristic-vs-heuristic (96 games, seed 1): steps 52,708 → 48,354, `RunInitiated` 2,954 → 2,775, `RunSucceeded` 2,707 → 2,543, `IceEncountered` 487 → 426, `SubroutineBroken` 239 → 174 against `SubroutineFired` 549 → 499, `AgendaStolen` 217 → 208, `AgendaScored` 203 → 200; outcomes Corp points 45 → 42, Runner points 46 → 47, flatlines 5 → 7. A small, plausible shift: nine of the 53 Corp cards and seven of the 43 Runner cards in the registry are identities, so about one hidden slot in six was a phantom with no cost, type or text the evaluator could price; a sample no longer wastes those slots. **But every one of those deltas sits inside the seed-spread band below**, so this is attribution (the change caused these exact games to go this way), not evidence of a systematic effect. Random-vs-random is byte-identical to the previous report, as it must be.
- **The seed-spread band — what a heuristic delta has to beat** (`docs/heuristic-noise-band`, September 2026). Reproducibility buys *attribution*, not *significance*: any code change re-rolls all 96 games, so a delta between two versions can be pure trajectory drift. Measured on `main` at `fe5ee02`, heuristic-vs-heuristic, 96 games each, seeds 1–5 (`target/coverage/noise-hh-seed{1..5}.json`):
  | metric | seed 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | range |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | steps | 48,354 | 48,256 | 56,665 | 53,256 | 58,221 | 9,965 (19%) |
  | `RunInitiated` | 2,775 | 3,033 | 3,585 | 3,150 | 3,652 | 877 (27%) |
  | `RunSucceeded` | 2,543 | 2,803 | 3,295 | 2,883 | 3,332 | 789 (27%) |
  | `IceEncountered` | 426 | 428 | 521 | 527 | 530 | 104 (21%) |
  | `SubroutineFired` | 499 | 527 | 601 | 582 | 654 | 155 (27%) |
  | `SubroutineBroken` | 174 | 161 | 232 | 257 | 168 | 96 (48%) |
  | `AgendaStolen` | 208 | 219 | 219 | 226 | 238 | 30 (14%) |
  | `AgendaScored` | 200 | 177 | 201 | 196 | 202 | 25 (13%) |
  | `ProgramInstalled` | 132 | 135 | 126 | 131 | 125 | 10 (8%) |
  | `CardInstalled` | 1,379 | 1,279 | 1,401 | 1,432 | 1,433 | 154 (11%) |
  | flatlines | 7 | 6 | 8 | 6 | 8 | 2 |
  | Corp point wins | 42 | 41 | 44 | 44 | 43 | 3 |
  | Runner point wins | 47 | 49 | 44 | 46 | 45 | 5 |

  Two things to read off it. **Event counts swing by a fifth to a half between seeds while the outcome split moves by three to five games** — so a single-seed claim about *how* the bots played (runs, encounters, breaks) needs a delta well clear of these ranges, and the outcome split is the tighter yardstick. And the run-to-run drift the pool sort removed was an order of magnitude smaller than this (steps ±969, `RunInitiated` ±83, everything else within ±10 on the same seed) — the nondeterminism was never the main source of noise, seed choice was. The structural deltas above (subroutines fired 1,006 → 493, Runner draws 1 → 388, Carmen 0 → 22, flatlines 58 → 6) clear the band by an order of magnitude; mid-sized ones in the same entries (e.g. `IceRezzed` 941 → 986, `AgendaStolen` 227 → 200) do not, and should be read as attribution rather than effect. A change claiming a small heuristic effect shows it across seeds, or not at all.
- Across the six heuristic branches of September 2026, heuristic-vs-heuristic went from 0.7 programs per game, 86 subroutines broken against 1,006 fired and 58 flatlines in 96 games, to 1.3 programs per game, 199 broken against 519 fired and 5 flatlines, with the Corp click-drawing, rezzing its assets and scoring behind ICE. The evaluator is still one-ply and `PuctAgent` is still what training uses; what remains is personality work (Phase 3 §1), not blindness.
- [x] **Retrained on the new evaluator, and the result is not to be trusted either — measured, September 2026** (`feat/puct-onnx-seat-and-retrain`). Six iterations of `scripts/run_iteration_loop.py` (96 games, 64 simulations, 10 epochs each; ~14 minutes an iteration once the network sits inside the search) on a clean corpus — the layout-v1 games from August (policy targets of 1,357 entries against today's `ActionSpace::SIZE` of 1,646) were moved to `data/selfplay_stale_layout_v1/` so the loop's cumulative training could not mix them in. **What happened:** iteration 1, played by the uniform search, split 52 Corp wins to 44 Runner with no stalls (median 173 decisions a game); the network trained on it drove iteration 2 to 86 Runner wins against 6, and every iteration after trained on more of the same — 92, 93, 90, 94 Runner wins out of 96. The value head's loss fell to 0.006 by learning "the Runner wins". Nothing in the loop pushes back: a checkpoint is promoted unconditionally, and the trainer's 90/10 split is over *steps*, so a game's neighbouring positions sit on both sides of it and a loss can fall by memorising games rather than judging positions.
  - **Measured against `main-puct-puct`** (24 games, all matchups, 32 simulations; the reference was run without `--seed`, these with `--seed 1`, so the comparison is distributional). The new `puct-onnx` bot kind — `PuctAgent` searching over the trained network, the shape self-play plays — makes the trained side seatable in `--headless`; it was not before (only the bare policy head, index path only). Uniform PUCT on the new evaluator versus the reference: stalls 14 → 2 of 24, steps 142,893 → 30,543, `RunInitiated` 35 → 536, `IceEncountered` 0 → 60, `ProgramInstalled` 9 → 35, `AgendaScored` 28 → 51, `AgendaStolen` 10 → 43 — the evaluator work reached the search agent. **The trained network is worse than the uniform search it replaced on both sides:** trained Corp vs uniform Runner 3–16 (11 of the 16 by the Corp decking itself out; `AgendaScored` 2 in 24 games); uniform Corp vs trained Runner 11–4 with 9 stalls and `ProgramInstalled` 1; trained vs trained 0–22, 14 by deck-out, `AgendaScored` 0. `checkpoints/latest_policy.onnx` is that network; `data/selfplay/iter_00{1..6}` is its corpus.
  - **What a trustworthy run needs, in order:** gate promotion on a head-to-head against the incumbent (AlphaZero's evaluator step — promote only above a win-rate threshold, which would have refused every checkpoint after the first); split train/validation by game, not by step; and only then more iterations.
  - **The first two are done — `feat/arena-promotion-gating`, September 2026.** `netrunner_selfplay --arena-candidate <onnx> [--arena-incumbent <onnx>] -n 48 -s 64` plays the candidate against the incumbent (the uniform search until something is promoted) from both chairs and prints one JSON line; the loop promotes only at `--promote-threshold` (0.55, wins plus half the stalls) and keeps a refused checkpoint as `rejected_iter_NNN.onnx`, every verdict in `checkpoints/promotions.log`. The trainer's validation split is by game (`split_by_game`), with at least one game held out. **Measured, two three-iteration runs (96 games, 64 simulations, 48 arena games each):** gating alone — verdicts 0.31, 0.30, 0.32, all refused, and self-play stayed at 52–44, 49–47, 49–47 with no stalls, where the ungated run had gone to 86–6 by iteration 2; gating plus the per-game split — verdicts 0.24, 0.40, 0.34, all refused, self-play 50–46, 49–47, 47–49, no stalls. The honest validation loss is 4.3–5.1 against the 2.2 the step split reported on the same corpora, and it rises from the first epoch: at this corpus size the network memorises games rather than judging positions, which is also why no candidate beats the search it was distilled from. What remains is the third item — more data per iteration before the split stops overfitting — and it is training work, not engine work. Reproducing this report is one command per seating (`--corp puct-onnx --runner puct --model ...`); the four reports here were produced as 24 parallel single-game runs (game *i*: seed 1+*i*, matchup *i* mod 12, the exact rotation `--all-matchups --seed 1` plays) and merged, because a stalled PUCT game is 10,000 steps of search and the sequential driver spends most of its time in those.
  - **The third — more data per iteration — measured, and the loop made able to carry it** (`feat/selfplay-volume`, September 2026). Three things stood between the loop and volume, each found by measuring rather than reading:
    - **Every un-promoted iteration replayed the previous one's games exactly.** A game's seed was its index, and since `determinize`'s pools were sorted self-play is bit-reproducible (checked: two runs of `-n 4` byte-identical), so a loop whose incumbent never changes regenerates the same corpus each iteration — "cumulative" training was the same 96 games repeated, with copies of one game on both sides of the per-game split. The run above escaped only because it predated the pool sort. Now: `--seed-offset` on `netrunner_selfplay` (the loop passes `(iteration − 1) × games`), the seed recorded in every trajectory, and the trainer refusing a corpus with a repeated seed.
    - **The trajectory format was 97–99% zeros written as text.** 990 observation floats with ~29 nonzero, 1,646 policy floats with ~8: 1.9 MB per game, and the trainer held every zero as a `float32`. Both vectors are now `[index, value]` pairs with the widths in the game header (a corpus mixing layouts is refused — August's 1,357-wide targets, caught by the reader rather than moved aside by hand); 96 games went from 190 MB to 11 MB, and the trainer densifies a batch at a time from CSR arrays. 2,400 games are 301 MB on disk, load in 18 s and train at 21 s an epoch on the RTX 3060.
    - **`--window N`** on the trainer and the loop — AlphaZero's replay window, the last N iterations rather than every game since the start — and the policy target's entropy printed beside the validation policy loss, so "4.3" reads as "1.6 nats above the 1.46 the target's own entropy sets". The loop is resumable (an iteration directory that already holds its games is not replayed) and writes one JSON line per iteration to `checkpoints/iterations.log` with timings and both summaries.
    - **Measured: one 2,400-game uniform-search corpus (seeds 0–2399, 14 minutes to generate, 535,419 decisions, 1,188 Corp wins to 1,206, 6 stalls), trained on its first 96, 288, 960 and 2,400 games, 10 epochs each, same split seed.** Best validation policy loss above the floor: **1.66 → 1.39 → 1.18 → 1.06 nats** — the policy head generalises better with every step of data and has not levelled off. The value head does not move: best validation MSE 1.23 / 1.04 / 1.18 / 1.30 against exactly 1.00 for predicting zero, with its training loss at 0.01–0.05 at every size. Bucketed by position in the game, held-out sign accuracy rises from 54% in the opening decile to 66% in the last, so the head carries a signal that grows late but is saturated (tanh) on memorised outcomes — overconfident on a weak signal, and 25× more data did not change that. The "rises from the first epoch" in the entry above was the value head all along; the policy's held-out loss falls for the first two or three epochs at every size.
    - **What that means:** more data is the right lever for the policy and the wrong one for the value head. Candidates, in order of cost: weight the value loss below the policy's or regularise the head separately; train it on the search's root value rather than the final outcome (the outcome of a 64-simulation uniform-search game is largely noise from the opening); stronger self-play. None was done here — the run below tests whether the policy gain alone produces a promotable candidate.
    - **Not the bottleneck: ONNX Runtime's thread pool.** Suspected because a search with the network runs ~6× slower than the uniform evaluator (16 games: 3,602 decisions in 33 s, against ~18,000 in 30 s uniform — ~2 ms per evaluation of a 990→256→256→1,646 MLP). Pinning each session to one intra-/inter-op thread was built and measured on the same 16 games: 33 s → 41 s. Reverted. The cost is per-call overhead or the encoding around it. Separately, stalled games — 10,000 decisions where a normal game is ~170 — dominate any arena that has them (2 of 4 games in a spot check), and the trained side stalls because `PuctAgent::select_action` has no cycle break, unlike self-play's `choose_action`. Both open.
    - **The 16-iteration run, stopped after five — measured, 2 September 2026.** `run_iteration_loop.py -i 16 -g 2400 -s 64 -e 10 --window 4 --arena-games 48`: every candidate refused, arena scores 0.27, 0.42, 0.34, 0.43, 0.375 against the 0.55 threshold (`checkpoints_uniform_volume_v1/promotions.log`). Two readings settled it early. **The value head did not move at any iteration**: held-out MSE 1.06–1.30 against exactly 1.00 for predicting zero, training loss 0.01–0.05 — the scaling measurement above, reproduced four more times at 2,400–9,600 games. **And the arena was measuring stalls as much as strength**: 14, 20, 17, 23 and 14 of every 48 games were draws, i.e. `Session` hit `MAX_STEPS`, each one 10,000 decisions of network search (arenas took 10–22 minutes) and each one half a point the candidate had not earned. By iteration 5 the window held 9,600 games, the policy loss above the floor had settled at 0.92–1.16 and was no longer falling, so the remaining eleven iterations would have re-measured the same two facts. Conclusion: uniform-search data alone does not produce a candidate that beats the uniform search, and the two items below had to land before another run was worth the machine. Its corpus and checkpoints are under `data/selfplay_uniform_volume_v1/` and `checkpoints_uniform_volume_v1/` (sparse format, no `search_value`, so the trainer accepts it only at `--value-target-mix 0`). The run before it is under `data/selfplay_dense_arena_gating_v1/` and `checkpoints_arena_gating_v1/`, in the old dense format the trainer no longer reads.
  - **`PuctAgent::select_action` had no cycle break, and the arena was paying for it — DONE** (`fix/puct-select-cycle-break`, September 2026). Self-play's driver has broken greedy cycles since the toggle two-cycle was found (`MAX_GREEDY_REPEATS`), but that lived in `netrunner_selfplay`; the `BotAgent` path — every `Seat::Agent(PuctAgent)`, which is both arena chairs and the `puct`/`puct-onnx` headless bots — was bare argmax-visits. The mechanism now lives where bot logic belongs, as `netrunner_bots::pick_action` with the constant beside it, and self-play's `choose_action` is a wrapper adding only the temperature schedule. Two decisions, both on the function's doc comment: **the escape strikes the repeated action from the candidates** and samples the rest by visits (uniformly if none was visited), where the driver used to sample *all* of them by visits — a confident network puts nearly every visit on its favourite, so that draw re-picked it with probability ≈ 1 and the "escape" was another lap; and the agent counts repeats per agent over its own decisions, since it never sees the opponent's, accepting that a long legitimate `PassPriority` chain can cost one non-pass pick. The uniform evaluator does not cycle on the Tāo fixture (its visits land nearly flat), which is why the stalls were on the trained side; the regression test (`netrunner_session/tests/puct_seat.rs`) drives the fixture with a toggle-loving evaluator standing in for an overfit network, and failed at seed 0 on the old code.
    - **Measured — iteration 1's arena replayed on the same 48 seeds with the same candidate:** draws 14 → **2**, wall 677 s → **374 s**, and the score 0.271 → **0.208** (9–37–2): the honest verdict, once stalls stop paying half a point each. What remains of the 374 s is the network in the search (~6× the uniform evaluator per decision, still open above). Both self-play stall counts are unchanged in kind — the driver already had the break — so the recorded trajectories differ only where an escape used to sample by visits.
    - `PuctSearchStats` also grew `root_value`, the mean value backed up through the root's edges from the agent's side — the search's own estimate of the position, recorded by self-play as `SelfPlayStep::search_value` for the entry below.
    - **`MctsAgent::select_action` was the same shape and got the same fix** in the same branch, through the same `pick_action`. Measured (12 headless MCTS-vs-MCTS games, seed 1): 0 stalls before, 0 after, 20.3 s → 19.1 s — the uniform-rollout search does not cycle on its own, so this one is defensive, the way the deferred-trigger and discard re-check entries were.
  - **The value head's target and weight — DONE, and the measurement found something bigger** (`fix/puct-select-cycle-break`, September 2026). The trainer gained `--value-target-mix λ` (target = (1−λ)·outcome + λ·`search_value`, both from the acting side; a corpus without `search_value` is refused at λ > 0) and `--value-loss-weight w` (the gradient's value term and checkpoint selection use `w`; the reported losses stay unweighted so runs compare), plus diagnostics that do not depend on the training target — held-out MSE against the *outcome* beside the predict-zero baseline, sign accuracy over decided games, and sign accuracy by game decile, the analysis the scaling entry did by hand. The loop passes both flags through.
    - **Measured on a fresh 960-game uniform-search corpus (seeds 0–959, 64 simulations, 272,277 decisions, 532 Corp wins to 424, 4 stalls), 10 epochs each, same split:**
      | λ | w | value MSE vs outcome (predict-zero 1.00) | sign accuracy | policy loss over floor |
      |---|---|---|---|---|
      | 0 | 1.0 (the old trainer) | 1.167 | 64.7% | 1.33 |
      | 0 | 0.25 | 1.128 | 65.9% | 1.35 |
      | 0.5 | 1.0 | 0.934 | 63.5% | 1.34 |
      | **0.5** | **0.25** | **0.896** | **66.1%** | 1.35 |
      | 1.0 | 1.0 | 1.140 | 46.3% | 1.27 |
      | 1.0 | 0.25 | 1.154 | 46.7% | 1.27 |
      The half-and-half target is the first setting whose head beats predicting zero on held-out games, and with the lighter weight it also has the best sign accuracy; those are the new defaults. The policy head is indifferent to all of it (1.27–1.35), so the memorising value head was *not* dragging the trunk down. Every cell still peaks at epoch 1–3 — the head's overfitting is slowed, not cured.
    - **But the root value predicts nothing about the outcome, and finding out why found a search bug and then a fact about the games.** Trained on it alone (λ = 1) the head is *below chance* on the outcome's sign, and that is in the data: across the corpus `search_value` has correlation **−0.037** with the signed outcome and agrees with its sign 46.9% of the time, flat from the opening decile (52%) to the last (48%). Not a perspective bug — `root_value_is_the_mean_backed_up_value_and_favors_a_winning_position` pins the sign on a one-move win, and `Side::Corp as u8 == 0` as the trajectory assumes. Two things came out of chasing it:
      - **Neither search negated at opponent nodes — FIXED** (same branch). `puct::simulate` and `mcts::simulate` carry the agent's `side` down the whole tree and `select_edge`/`uct` maximised Q *from that side* at every node, so at the opponent's decision the search descended the move best for the *agent*: the opponent as an accomplice, every backed-up value the value of a fantasy line. Both now flip the sign of the exploitation term where `current_actor` is the opponent (`agent_to_move`); values stay in the agent's perspective, since the evaluator's value already is. Pinned by `select_edge_minimises_the_agents_value_where_the_opponent_decides` and `uct_flips_sign_where_the_opponent_decides`. **Measured, and it changes less than expected at these budgets:** against the unchanged heuristic (24 games a chair, seed 1, the CLI's 32 simulations) uniform PUCT went 8+3 → 7+3 wins of 48 and MCTS 4+11 → 5+12; uniform PUCT self-play at 64 simulations ends 10/7/4/3 → 9/6/6/3 (Corp points / flatline / Runner points / Corp deck-out) over 24 games; and the regenerated 960-game corpus has `search_value` correlation with the outcome of **−0.084**, no better. A real defect, corrected because a search that models the opponent as helpful cannot be trusted at any budget, but not what made the root value useless.
      - **What did: the score margin itself does not predict these games.** The root value tracks the agenda-point margin almost exactly (correlation **0.92**, sign agreement 94% wherever the margin is non-zero, before and after negamax) — the search values what it should. But in uniform-search self-play the margin's sign agrees with the outcome only **45–47%** of the time, and at each game's *last recorded decision* the side ahead on points won 394 of 739 decided games (53%). About 40% of these games end by flatline or Corp deck-out, endings the margin does not see, and the side that has been scoring or stealing is the one nearest them — a Corp ahead on points has been drawing through R&D, a Runner ahead has been running rather than drawing — which is where the small *negative* correlation comes from. The value target the ROADMAP wanted ("the search's root value rather than the final outcome") is therefore a smooth, position-specific, *heuristic* signal, and mixing it in helps as a regulariser against memorising game labels, not as a better proxy for who wins. Neither target can teach a value head much until self-play is strong enough that positions decide games; that is the third remedy on the list above, and the one left.
    - **Grid re-run on the negamax corpus (960 games, seeds 0–959, 239,998 decisions, 531 Corp wins to 426, 3 stalls):** λ 0/w 1.0 (old trainer) MSE 1.507, sign 55.9%; λ 0/w 0.25 → 1.401, 57.6%; **λ 0.5/w 0.25 → 1.031, 57.3%**; λ 0.5/w 1.0 → 1.089, 55.9%; λ 1.0 → 1.19–1.22 at 45%. Every outcome-only cell is worse than on the first corpus (1.13–1.17 → 1.40–1.51: more confident memorisation of a noisier label), and the same cell wins on MSE with sign accuracy within a point of the best, so the defaults stay **λ = 0.5, w = 0.25**. The policy head is again indifferent (1.20–1.30 over the floor). **In the arena the two are indistinguishable:** the λ 0.5/w 0.25 network and the old-trainer network each score 10–37–1 (0.219) against the uniform search over the same 48 seeds — the better-calibrated head does not yet make a stronger player, which is the diagnosis above stated as a result. The relaunched run carries the new defaults because they are measurably no worse on the outcome and materially better calibrated; whether a value head can matter at all now depends on stronger self-play.
  - **The second 2,400-game run: nine iterations, every candidate refused, and a harness that let the engine change underneath it — measured, 2–3 September 2026** (`fix/training-loop-pins-its-engine`). `run_iteration_loop.py -i 16 -g 2400 -s 64 -e 10 --window 4 --arena-games 48 --value-target-mix 0.5 --value-loss-weight 0.25`, launched 2 September 13:27 from `main` at 3229e83 with the cycle break, negamax and mixed value target above. It **did not reach iteration 16**: it ran nine iterations in 6.0 h and died at iteration 10's arena, and the reason was not a training fact. Arena scores **0.302 (14–33–1), 0.229 (10–36–2), 0.323 (15–32–1), 0.271 (13–35–0), 0.375 (17–29–2), 0.281 (13–34–1), 0.427 (19–26–3), 0.281 (13–34–1), 0.302 (14–33–1)** against the 0.55 threshold — mean 0.31, no trend across a window that grew to 9,600 games.
    - **The cycle break delivered exactly what it promised.** Arena draws **14–23 of 48 → 0–3 of 48** and arena wall 10–22 min → 4–5 min, so a score is now the candidate's record rather than half a point per stalled game. (Iteration 4's arena took 31 s against 4–5 min for every other one and is unexplained; a pinned binary and a recorded engine identity are what would have made it attributable, which is half the point of the entry below.)
    - **Nothing was ever promoted, so no iteration ever had a network in its self-play** — `latest_policy.onnx` was never written, and every one of the ten iterations is the *uniform* search on fresh seeds. That reframes the result: this was not an AlphaZero loop closing, it was 24,000 more uniform-search games asking whether volume alone produces a candidate that beats the search that generated it. It does not. Validation policy loss above the floor stayed at 0.94–1.36 with no downward trend (1.17 at iteration 1, 1.19 at iteration 9), against 1.06 measured on 2,400 games in the scaling entry above — the policy gain from more data had already been collected.
    - **The value head did not improve, and the number that says it did is an artefact.** `mse_vs_outcome` falls 0.993 → 0.737 across the run, which reads as progress until `baseline_mse` — predicting zero, i.e. the decided-step fraction — is read beside it: it falls too, 1.000 → 0.767. The ratio is 0.99, 1.07, 0.97, 0.92, 0.98, 0.91, 0.97, 0.96, 1.02 — the head beats predicting zero by a few percent at best and loses to it at iterations 2 and 9. Held-out sign accuracy 0.596 → 0.616, with the last decile 0.66 → 0.70, is the whole real gain. The diagnosis from the grid stands unchanged: no value target can teach the head much while 40% of games end by flatline or deck-out and the margin does not predict them.
    - **Why the baseline moved is the finding worth keeping: stalled games are ~24% of every training corpus.** At iteration 8, **19 games of 2,400 (0.8%) held 187,971 of ~780,000 recorded decisions**, mean 9,893 steps against 251 for a game that ends — `MAX_STEPS` of cycling near-duplicate positions, each carrying a zero value target and a policy target from a search that is looping. The trainer now drops any game whose recorded `end_reason` starts with `stall_` and prints how many decisions went with it; self-play records that reason from the terminal `SessionStep` (`GameTrajectory::end_reason`). A stall is not a draw: the arena stopped paying half a point for one when the cycle break landed, and the trainer stops paying for one now.
    - **How it actually died: the loop shelled out to `cargo run` for every stage, so each stage rebuilt the engine from the working tree.** *Elevation* was being implemented stage by stage in the same tree while the run played. `overnight.log` records 20-second recompiles before iterations 8 and 9's self-play, and at 20:02 iteration 10's arena compiled a half-finished edit (`IceBypassed`, `bypass_encountered_ice`) and exited 101, taking six iterations with it. **The silent half is worse than the crash**: read off the recorded `matchup` ids, the self-play deck pool went **12 matchups (iterations 1–7) → 20 (iteration 8) → 36 (iterations 9–10)**, and `observation.rs::set_rank` ranked `elev` *ahead* of `core`, so every Elevation card that landed pushed all 19 Core Set cards along their planes (77..=95 before it). The trainer's four-iteration window mixed three pools and two encodings and objected to none of it, because the only thing it compared was the recorded widths, and `OBS_SIZE` and `ActionSpace::SIZE` never moved. **This is the third time recorded data has gone stale under the engine**, after the blank-filler fixture and the pre-icebreaker corpus, and the first time it happened *during* the run rather than before it.
    - **Three fixes, each aimed at a different half of that.** (1) **A run is pinned to one binary**: `run_iteration_loop.py` builds once, copies the executable to `<ckpt-dir>/bin/`, and invokes that path for every self-play and arena stage — no `cargo` after the initial build, so neither a working-tree edit nor another session's target lock can reach a run in flight. Each `iterations.log` line now carries the commit, whether the tree was dirty, and the binary's sha256; a stage failure appends a `"failed"` line and prints the `--start-iter` command that resumes, where the dead run left nine lines and no tenth. (2) **A corpus that mixes engines is refused**, the third such refusal beside mixed widths and duplicate seeds: `netrunner_core::pool_fingerprint()` is an FNV-1a hash over every playable card and every sample deck as their JSON, recorded in each trajectory as `pool_fingerprint`; an absent value is the archived corpora's own, so they still load. It catches card and deck data moving, not a rules change in Rust — the pinned binary is what covers that, and the two are meant to be read together. (3) **`set_rank` now orders `sg`, `core`, `elev`**, the order sets actually entered the vocabulary, so a new set appends instead of inserting; `vocabulary_order_is_stable` pins the Core Set's *slots* rather than the tail's ids, which is what let the reindex pass, and a new test asserts every set's slots sit above every earlier set's.
    - The corpus and checkpoints are archived to `data/selfplay_mixed_pool_v2/` and `checkpoints_mixed_pool_v2/`. Iterations 1–7 are one pool, 8–10 are not; the whole thing is readable but not trainable as one window, which is the state the fingerprint exists to make loud. No coverage report accompanies this branch: the only `netrunner_core` addition is a pure fingerprint function, no rules path is touched, and both 256-seed sweeps ran clean.
    - **What the run says to do next**, and what the third attempt tests: the bottleneck is self-play strength, not volume. 24,000 uniform-search games did not produce a promotable candidate, and the value head cannot learn from games whose positions do not decide them. The third run therefore trades games for search — `-i 16 -g 1200 -s 128` — on a pinned binary, with the fingerprint in every trajectory and stalls dropped from every window.
  - Original note, kept for the record: **every self-play game recorded before this predated working icebreakers.** Until the memory-cost fix, no Runner in any recorded game could install a program, so the value head learned Runner play with no rig and the policy head never saw an `InstallProgram` slot chosen. The memory feature in `observation.rs` was constant across the whole corpus. Nothing structural changed — `OBS_SIZE`, the card vocabulary and `ActionSpace::SIZE` are all unmoved, so an exported model still loads — but its inputs now vary where they never did.
  - **This is the second time recorded data has gone stale under the engine**, after the blank-filler fixture in §3 above whose 5,000 games all ended in a Corp loss. Same lesson: a training corpus is only as good as the engine that generated it, and neither failure announced itself.

- **The third run, stopped at nine iterations, and what stopping it to *look* found — 4 September 2026.** `-i 16 -g 1200 -s 128` ran to iteration 9 with **8 of 8 candidates refused** (0.427, 0.312, 0.479, 0.427, 0.406, 0.375, 0.219, 0.396 against the 0.55 bar). Aggregated that is **141–233–10 of 384, score 0.380 ± 0.049, about −85 Elo**: the candidates are not missing a noisy bar, they are decisively worse than the search that generated their data. Killed deliberately; all 10,800 games and 8 rejected checkpoints kept.
  - **The arena can now seat half a network** (`netrunner_bots::SplitEvaluator`, `netrunner_selfplay --candidate-uses both|value-only|priors-only`). A `PolicyEvaluator` answers two questions at once and one score cannot say which went wrong. Ablation over the *candidate* only — the incumbent is the bar, and moving it makes two runs incomparable.
  - **Measured on `rejected_iter_008.onnx` at 128 simulations, first at 48 games and then re-run at 192.** The n=48 control reproduced iteration 8 exactly (18–28–2, 0.3958333, 1026s against its recorded 1050s), so the differences were the ablation's — but it could not separate the two halves (z ≈ 1.50, p ≈ 0.13), and it was measured *before* the alias fix below, which changed how prior mass is distributed across the mask: the exact quantity under test. The re-run on the fixed engine is the one to read.

    | candidate uses | W–L–D (n=192) | score | 95% CI | Elo | wall | n=48 score |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | both (control) | 60–127–5 | 0.3255 | ±0.065 | −127 | 917s | 0.3958 |
    | value-only (uniform priors) | 72–105–15 | **0.4141** | ±0.067 | −60 | 2999s | 0.4375 |
    | priors-only (heuristic value) | 36–156–0 | **0.1875** | ±0.055 | −255 | 266s | 0.2917 |

    **The priors are the harmful half, and it is no longer close: value-only − priors-only = 0.227, z = 5.12, p ≈ 3 × 10⁻⁷.** Every score moved down 0.02–0.10 under the alias fix but the ordering held, so what n=48 saw directionally was real. Keeping only the network's value recovers ~67 Elo of the whole network's −127; keeping only its priors costs a further −128. **The value head is not innocent** — value-only's interval tops out at 0.481, still short of parity — but that is Finding 2's lossy-observation problem rather than a bug, and it is a quarter the size of the priors' damage. **Fix the policy perspective before spending another night on the loop.**
  - **What n=192 buys and what it still does not.** 192 games is one full pass of the matchup cross product, so where n=48 reached 4 of 16 Corp decks this reaches every deck once. The chair confound survives: `matchup_for` is `game_index % 192` and `candidate_side` is `game_index % 2`, so each matchup is still played from one fixed chair. That is identical across all three legs, so the comparison holds; what it costs is the per-chair split, which `ArenaSummary` still does not emit and which is the most diagnostic number available. **Throughput, same command shape as the n=48 run:** 4× the games in 0.9× the wall for `both` (1026s → 917s) and 1.4× for `value-only` (2104s → 2999s) — the alias fix and cycle break measured end to end on the arena rather than on self-play.
  - **The mechanism it is testing, all three legs verified by reading.** `OnnxPolicyEvaluator` encodes from a side fixed at construction (`onnx_policy.rs:60`) while `get_action_mask` returns the *side-to-move*'s legal set, and training only ever pairs a side's observation with that same side's visit counts. **91.7% of the 1,646 `ActionSpace` slots are exclusive to one side** (segments sum exactly to `SIZE`); `encode_view` is literally "self against opponent", swapped by side; and `phase_one_hot` discards the side from every phase, so the observation carries no whose-turn feature to disambiguate with. At an opponent node the softmax therefore renormalizes over the slots training drove toward −∞.
  - **Two corrections to claims made while investigating, both recorded because the reasoning was wrong, not just the number.** (1) The stalls are *not* a two-sided alternating cycle defeating a shared counter — every one of iteration 9's twenty stalls is one-sided, 99.1% `ToggleCardSelection` over nine slots, with `ConfirmCardSelection` legal and picked 0.46% of the time. (2) A byte-identical 192-matchup coverage report does **not** clear a `get_action_mask` change: `--headless` random-vs-random drives `BotAgent` off `view.legal_actions` and never calls the mask. Measuring the path that does — 24 self-play games, same seeds — showed 21 of 24 diverging.

- **A hand is a multiset, and the `ActionSpace` did not know it — DONE** (`fix/action-space-aliases`, 4 September 2026). Found by that divergence. `candidate_actions` proposes one action per hand position and a hand card is named by `CardId`, so three copies of *Hedge Fund* in HQ produced **three byte-identical `PlayOperation` entries, six mask bits and a tripled share of a uniform prior**. Two installed copies genuinely differ — different counters, which is why Phase 1 §3 introduced `InstallId` — but two copies in hand are the same move. The hand side never got that treatment.
  - It reached everything downstream: `ClientView::legal_actions` showed a player the same entry N times; `UniformPolicyEvaluator` divides by `legal_count`, so a multi-copy card drew N times the prior mass **in every search this repo has run, including the incumbent that beat all 27 candidates**; a random agent was biased toward multi-copy cards.
  - `legal_actions` now deduplicates, which fixes the first three at the source. The fourth needed `get_action_mask` built **forwards** through `index_of` — one bit per action at its canonical slot — because the backwards form decoded all three positions back to the same legal action even over a deduplicated list. That is also `O(legal)` rather than `O(SIZE × legal)` with a `String` compare in the inner loop.
  - `assert_mask_matches_legal_actions` stays written backwards from `action_at` and gains the canonical-slot clause, so it remains an independent derivation and the only assertion that `index_of` and `action_at` still invert each other.
  - **Measured: 162 → 527 recorded decisions per second (3.25×)** on 24 self-play games at 64 simulations, identical seeds. Coverage (192 matchups, random-vs-random, seed 1) deliberately **not** byte-identical — this changes what a bot is offered: steps 73,377 → 73,610 (+0.3%), outcomes move 14 of 192, `ActivateAbility` 3,362 → 3,012 and `CountersAdded` 3,284 → 2,836 as duplicate ability entries stop being over-drawn. Both 256-seed sweeps clean.
  - **Note for whoever trains next:** no slot moved, so existing checkpoints stay structurally valid, but their priors were fit against the aliased mask.

- **The cycle break strikes the set, not the last action — DONE** (`fix/cycle-escape-strikes-the-set`, 4 September 2026). `MAX_GREEDY_REPEATS` escapes a run of one identical action; `pick_action` then removes that action and samples the rest by visits, and when the cycle runs over interchangeable moves the rest *is* the cycle. `CycleGuard` (shared by `PuctAgent`, `MctsAgent` and self-play's driver) keeps a window of the last `MAX_GREEDY_REPEATS + 1` picks and strikes the whole distinct set when it holds at most `MAX_CYCLE_WIDTH` (2) of them — a strict generalisation, since a window of one distinct action fires on exactly the pick the counter fired on.
  - **`pick_action`'s escape degrades in three rungs and the middle one is load-bearing:** strike the set; if nothing legal survives, strike only the most recent pick; only then everything. Without it this was *worse* than what it replaced — `a_puct_seat_does_not_toggle_a_card_selection_until_the_budget_runs_out` regressed at seed 2, because a selection mid-cycle does not satisfy its bound, `ConfirmCardSelection` is not offered, and striking every toggle collapsed to an unfiltered sample that landed back on the cycle's favourite.
  - **No stall reduction is claimed.** 96 games at 64 simulations, identical seeds, against `main` already carrying the alias fix: stalls 0 → 0, longest game 847 → 620 steps, total steps 24,798 → 23,267 (−6%). The alias fix had already taken this sample to zero stalls, and 64 simulations does not reproduce the s=128 corpus the toggle cycles were found in. Hardening against a failure mode observed in real data, in the same spirit as `MctsAgent`'s defensive break — and it reaches the arena, which self-play's driver never could. Coverage byte-identical, as it must be: `RandomAgent` never calls `pick_action`.


- **Both heads are asked from the side to move, and it changed nothing — DONE** (`fix/policy-head-perspective`, 4 September 2026). The ablation above named the priors as the harmful half, and the mechanism on offer was `OnnxPolicyEvaluator` encoding from a side fixed at construction while the tree evaluates nodes both sides decide. Fixed, measured, **and the hypothesis did not survive the measurement.**
  - **The finding that reshaped the fix is in the trainer, not the engine.** `train_alpha_netrunner.py:218` signs the value target by the acting side (`outcome_corp if active_side == 0 else -outcome_corp`), and self-play only ever encodes its observation from the awaiting side. So *every* training example — policy target and value target alike — is "this side is to move, from this side's perspective", and an observation from a side that is **not** to move is a shape the network has never seen. The first cut of this branch corrected the policy head and left the value head reading out of distribution at exactly the nodes it always had; the earlier claim in this section that "the value head's fixed perspective is correct by design" was wrong.
  - **The shipped form is one forward pass, not two.** Ask the network from `current_actor`'s perspective and negate the value when that is not the agent's side. `puct::simulate` still gets every value from one fixed side — the outcome is zero-sum, so value-from-opponent is exactly −value-from-self, which is the ordinary negamax identity. `current_actor` rather than `GamePhase`, because `phase` stays `Action(Runner)` for a whole run while a paid-ability window hands priority to the Corp to rez; a test pins that case.
  - **Measured, `rejected_iter_008.onnx`, 192 games, 128 simulations, against the same uniform incumbent and the same seeds as the entry above.** Two forms were run so the value half could be separated from the policy half:

    | candidate uses | unfixed | policy head only (2 passes) | both heads, negamax (1 pass) | delta | p |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | priors-only | 0.1875 | 0.1745 | **0.1745** | −0.013 | 0.74 |
    | both | 0.3255 | 0.2526 | **0.2865** | −0.039 | 0.39 |
    | value-only | 0.4141 | — | **0.4323** | +0.018 | 0.70 |

    **Nothing moved.** Every leg sits within noise of the unfixed baseline, and `both` — the only configuration a real match would seat — is nominally *worse*. Merged anyway, and claiming nothing, on the same grounds as the cycle break above: it is what the training distribution actually asks for, and it costs exactly the one forward pass the code it replaces cost. **The perspective bug was real and is not why the priors lose.**
  - **The arena is deterministic at fixed seeds, and this run proves it rather than assuming it.** `priors-only` came back bit-identical (33–158–1) from two different binaries, which share the policy path and differ only in the value path that leg discards. So these deltas are *attributable* — no run-to-run spread to hide behind — even though 192 games still bounds how far they generalise.
  - **How much of the tree the fix even reaches: 34.6%** — 2,674 of 7,739 evaluator calls over 60 plies of real PUCT-128 play are at opponent-to-move nodes. Measured with a counting `PolicyEvaluator`, so the null result is not "the code never fired".
  - **Wall time is not per-node cost here** and should not be read as one: `both` 917 s → 2490 s and `value-only` 2999 s → 7945 s, while the pass count per node is unchanged. Different values steer different games — `value-only`'s draws go 15 → 38 of 192 — and longer games are the whole increase. Worth knowing before sizing a training run around the arena.
  - **What this leaves pointing at the policy head.** The priors cost ~270 Elo against the value head's ~47, and perspective does not explain it. The next suspect is the objective itself: `train_alpha_netrunner.py:301-306` takes cross-entropy over all 1,646 slots with **no legality mask**, while inference renormalizes over the ~7 legal ones, so the quantity trained is not the quantity the search consumes and the network spends capacity learning a legality it cannot infer. The alias fix is *not* an excuse — `policy_target_for` always wrote its target to `index_of`'s canonical slot while the old mask marked every duplicate slot legal, so that fix moved inference **toward** the training target, not away from it.

- **The policy head diagnosed offline: it ranks better than chance and loses anyway, because it is overconfident — 4 September 2026** (`scripts/diagnose_policy_head.py`). The perspective fix above ruled out its own hypothesis without naming a replacement, so the next step was to measure the policy head directly instead of guessing at it again. **47,327 held-out steps** from iteration 9 — generated after `rejected_iter_008.onnx` was fitted and outside its four-iteration window, stalled games dropped as the trainer drops them.
  - **The script needs no `onnxruntime`.** The exported net is a plain MLP (Gemm / LayerNorm / Relu, two heads), so it reads the weights off the ONNX initializers with `onnx` — already a training dependency — and reproduces the forward pass in numpy. It takes `[games] [model] [corpus_dir]`, so it points at any checkpoint and any corpus.
  - **The head has learned something real.** Top-1 agreement with the search's own visit argmax is **44.4%** against **30.1%** for uniform — a 14-point lift. Split by chair it is 46.9% Corp against 41.6% Runner, the same direction as the arena asymmetry recorded further up.
  - **The unmasked objective is not the main story, and the measurement is why that theory was dropped rather than built on.** `train_alpha_netrunner.py:301-306` takes cross-entropy over all 1,646 slots with no legality mask, and the standing prediction was that the model would put nearly all its mass on illegal slots. It puts **58.8%** on *legal* ones (median 60.4%), and its unmasked argmax is already a legal slot **74.8%** of the time. Real waste, not a catastrophe.
  - **What actually loses: the prior is peakier than the target it was trained to imitate.**

    | | mean | median |
    |---|---|---|
    | model's top prior | **0.452** | 0.504 |
    | search's top visit share — the target | 0.349 | 0.391 |
    | uniform | 0.301 | — |

    Entropy is 0.832 of uniform's. So the head is *more* confident than the search it is distilling while being wrong 56% of the time, which is the signature of the overfitting `best_epoch` already reports (1–3 of 10 epochs, every iteration of the run).
  - **And an overconfident prior starves the correct move, with nothing in the search to catch it.** In the 56% of steps where the model is wrong, the search's actual best action gets a median prior of **0.110** while the model's own wrong pick gets 0.325; across all steps, **26.9%** give the correct action below 0.10 and 9.5% below 0.05. `puct_score`'s exploration term is linear in the prior (`puct.rs:165`) and **there is no root Dirichlet noise anywhere in `puct.rs`** — AlphaZero adds it at the root for exactly this failure — so a starved edge is never explored back. A uniform prior starves nothing, which is how a *worse* ranking beats a better one and why `priors-only` (0.1745) sits so far below `value-only` (0.4141).
  - **The dial that tests it:** `netrunner_bots::MixedPriorEvaluator` and `netrunner_selfplay --candidate-prior-mix`, mixing the candidate's priors toward uniform over the legal set (`P' = (1 − ε)P + ε/n`). An instrument like `SplitEvaluator`, never something to seat in a match, and applied after the ablation so it dials whichever priors were left in place. `ε = 1.0` turns the candidate's prior into the incumbent's exactly, which makes the top of the sweep a self-check rather than a leap of faith.
  - **Swept, `priors-only`, 192 games, 128 simulations each — and the starvation theory failed too.** If starvation were the mechanism, a little uniform mass would buy back a lot of score. It buys almost nothing until the network's prior is nearly gone:

    | ε (uniform share) | network share | score | wall |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | 0.00 | 100% | 0.1745 | 396s |
    | 0.25 | 75% | 0.1771 | 366s |
    | 0.50 | 50% | 0.2031 | 550s |
    | 0.75 | 25% | 0.2813 | 358s |
    | 1.00 | 0% | **0.5755** | 1170s |

    Monotone, with no sweet spot anywhere: **no mixing weight beats discarding the priors entirely.** Read from the top down, the *first* quarter of network prior costs 0.294 of score and the last three quarters cost 0.107 between them — the damage saturates, because once the network's peak dominates the mix it already owns the argmax. A head that agrees with the search 44.4% of the time against 30.1% for chance is nonetheless worth **less than nothing** to the search at every weight, which is the sharpest statement this section can make about distillation: ranking accuracy is not the quantity that matters, and no amount of calibration rescues it. `ε = 0.00` reproduced 33–158–1 across three separately-built binaries, so the column is attributable.
  - **The sweep's control found a bigger bug than the sweep: the arena hands the candidate +0.0755 for free.** At `ε = 1.0` the candidate's evaluator is *provably identical* to the incumbent's — uniform priors over the same mask, `evaluate_state` value, same weights — so the honest score is 0.5. It scored **0.5755 (109–80–3)**. That deviation cannot be sampling: the players are the same, 192 games is the **entire** matchup cross product played exactly once, and the seeds are fixed (`seed = game_index`), so this is the arena's exact, reproducible seating bias rather than an estimate of one.
    - **The mechanism is the chair confound, now quantified.** `decks::matchups()` builds the cross product as `corp_index * 12 + runner_index`, and 12 is even, so a matchup's index has the parity of its *Runner* deck. `candidate_side` is `game_index % 2`. The candidate therefore plays **Corp against the six even-indexed Runner decks and Runner as the six odd-indexed ones**, and never the other way — the doc comment claiming "every pairing is played from both sides" has been false since the pool stopped being 12 matchups.
    - **The 0.55 promotion gate is already cleared by a candidate identical to the incumbent** (0.5755 > 0.55). Nothing was ever promoted across three volume runs, so no bad model reached `latest_policy.onnx` — that is luck, not the gate working. ~~Every arena score in this section is inflated by about 0.0755.~~ **Corrected by the fix below** — the bias is exactly +0.0755 for a *null* candidate but is not an additive constant for a real one, because what was confounded was which matchups each chair drew, not how often each chair came up. Measured on the same binary and model, de-biasing moved `both` by +0.001, `priors-only` by −0.016 and `value-only` by −0.048.
    - Fix before the next verdict: decouple chair from matchup (rotate the chair on something coprime to the pool, or play each matchup twice, once from each chair), and give `ArenaSummary` the per-chair breakdown it still lacks. A null-candidate run at `ε = 1.0` is the regression test, and it must come back at 0.5.

- **The arena plays both chairs, and the verdict finally says which one loses — DONE** (`fix/arena-chair-confound`, 5 September 2026). The chair confound above is fixed, and fixing it immediately overturned this section's central finding.
  - **Chair is decoupled from matchup by pairing.** `arena_matchup_index` is `game_index / 2`, so games `2k` and `2k+1` are the same pairing from opposite chairs, and the arena's seed follows the *pair* rather than the game — both halves are one deal played both ways round. Self-play is untouched: `play_one_game` still indexes matchups on the game index, because there is no chair to balance when both seats are the same searcher and a corpus wants breadth. Full cross-product coverage now costs 384 games rather than 192.
  - **The regression test is exact, not statistical.** With candidate and incumbent the same evaluator a pair is one game scored from both sides, so it contributes exactly one win and one loss whatever happens in it: `a_candidate_identical_to_the_incumbent_scores_exactly_one_half` asserts `== 0.5` with no tolerance. Confirmed at full scale too — the null candidate returned **189–189–6 over 384 games**. `every_matchup_is_played_from_both_chairs` walks the whole cross product and pins same-matchup/opposite-chair, the property the old indexing silently violated.
  - **`ArenaSummary` now carries `as_corp` and `as_runner`**, and the null run shows why that field had to exist: with *identical players*, the Corp chair scores **0.742** and the Runner **0.258**. The sample pool under uniform PUCT-128 is 74% Corp-favoured, worth roughly 180 Elo before anyone plays — which is why any coupling between chair and matchup swamped the verdict, and it is the baseline every score below is read against.
  - **Re-measured on the unbiased arena, `rejected_iter_008.onnx`, 384 games, 128 simulations:**

    | candidate uses | overall | as Corp | vs chair baseline | as Runner | vs chair baseline | draws (C/R) |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | null control | **0.5000** | 0.7422 | — | 0.2578 | — | 3 / 3 |
    | both | 0.2878 | 0.2318 | **−0.510** | 0.3438 | **+0.086** | 25 / 4 |
    | priors-only | 0.1589 | 0.0755 | **−0.667** | 0.2422 | −0.016 | 1 / 1 |
    | value-only | 0.3841 | 0.4375 | −0.305 | 0.3307 | **+0.073** | 44 / 17 |

  - **This is the finding, and it rewrites the two above it: the network is broken as the Corp and fine-to-good as the Runner.** Read against the chair baseline, *both* heads are a small **improvement** on uniform from the Runner chair (+0.086 whole-network, +0.073 value, −0.016 priors) and a disaster from the Corp chair (−0.510, −0.305, −0.667). Every earlier verdict in this section — "the priors are the harmful half", "the head is worth less than nothing at every mixing weight" — was an average over two chairs that behave nothing alike, and the honest version is **the *Corp* priors are the harmful half**. It also explains why the ε sweep looked so flat: mixing uniform into a policy that is neutral on one chair and ruinous on the other can only trace the average of the two.
  - **The Corp failure mode is not losing, it is failing to close.** Draws as Corp run 25 of 192 whole-network and **44 of 192** value-only, against 4 and 17 as Runner. A stall is half a point from a chair that wins 74% of its games outright, so each one throws most of a point away. **A hypothesis, untested, recorded as such given this section's record:** the value target is signed by `active_side`, so a Corp step's target is `+1` in roughly three games of four — a Corp value head that learns to answer "winning" everywhere gives the search no gradient to prefer scoring an agenda over passing, and the game runs to `MAX_STEPS`. The Runner's mirror-image imbalance has no such consequence, because a Runner who believes it is losing still has to act.
  - **And it contradicts the offline diagnostic in a way worth chasing:** `scripts/diagnose_policy_head.py` puts Corp top-1 agreement *above* Runner's (46.9% against 41.6%). So the Corp policy imitates the search's first choice **better** and plays far worse, which means the defect is not the move it ranks first. The cheap next measurement is prior mass on the `ADVANCE_CARD`/`SCORE_AGENDA` blocks against the search's own — a Corp that never advances cannot win, and top-1 agreement would not notice.
  - No coverage report: nothing in `netrunner_core` is touched, and the change is to how an arena harness indexes its own games.

- **Where the Corp policy's mass actually goes: it will not score, will not rez, and passes instead — 5 September 2026** (`diag/policy-head-by-segment`). The chair split above said the Corp head is what loses and named "failing to close" as the failure mode. This measures it: for every `ActionSpace` segment, over the same 47,327 held-out steps, the mass the **search** put there against the mass the **model** gave the search, per chair, counted only over steps where that segment had a legal slot at all.
  - `scripts/diagnose_policy_head.py` grew the breakdown. The segment offsets are derived in the script because `action_mask.rs`'s consts are private, and the derivation **asserts against the pinned `ActionSpace::SIZE`** — if the lengths stop summing to 1,646 the layout moved and the table is stale, so it fails loudly instead of silently mislabelling. It prints every segment the search puts mass on rather than a top-N, which is the only reason the finding below is visible at all.
  - **The Corp, ordered by the search's own mass:**

    | segment | slots | steps | search | model | ratio |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | install card | 416 | 7377 | 0.620 | 0.509 | 0.82 |
    | pass priority | 2 | 7472 | 0.473 | **0.818** | **1.73** |
    | activate ability (corp) | 128 | 7299 | 0.351 | 0.109 | **0.31** |
    | rez ice | 32 | 5516 | 0.411 | 0.200 | **0.49** |
    | basic action | 9 | 10085 | 0.184 | 0.253 | 1.37 |
    | ADVANCE CARD | 32 | 4120 | 0.280 | 0.255 | 0.91 |
    | confirm card selection | 1 | 3452 | 0.241 | 0.411 | 1.70 |
    | gain credit | 2 | 8099 | 0.095 | 0.148 | 1.56 |
    | draw | 2 | 8090 | 0.090 | 0.146 | 1.62 |
    | **SCORE AGENDA** | 32 | **430** | **0.659** | **0.204** | **0.31** |

  - **`SCORE AGENDA` is the finding, and it is only legal on 430 of 24,548 Corp steps.** When it is, the search spends **two thirds** of its visits there and the model offers **a fifth** — a 3× under-weighting of the one action that ends the game in the Corp's favour. Scoring is rare enough that no aggregate metric can see it: Corp top-1 agreement is 46.9%, *higher* than the Runner's, because it is dominated by the thousands of ordinary steps. **A prediction half right, recorded as such:** the standing guess was that the Corp under-weights advancing *and* scoring. `ADVANCE CARD` is 0.91 — near-neutral, essentially innocent. Only the scoring half held.
  - **And the general shape is passivity.** Mass moves out of the wide, active segments — `activate ability` 0.31, `rez ice` 0.49, `install card` 0.82 — and into the narrow ones a player can always take: `pass priority` **1.73**, `draw` 1.62, `gain credit` 1.56, `basic action` 1.37. A Corp that rezzes half as often as it should, activates a third as often, refuses to score, and passes 73% more than it should is not a Corp that loses games; it is a Corp that never finishes them. That is precisely the 25-of-192 and 44-of-192 draw counts in the entry above.
  - **The Runner is the same distortion with none of the consequences,** which is the whole reason its chair holds up: its two dominant segments are barely touched — `basic action` 1.06, `initiate run` 1.10 — so the bias lands on `install resource` (0.43) and `play event` (0.61) while the Runner's core game is priced correctly. The Corp's *entire* game lives in the segments that got suppressed.
  - **What it points at.** The suppressed segments are the wide ones (128, 416, 32 slots) and the inflated ones are narrow (1, 2, 9); the model appears to hedge toward actions it can always name and away from choices among many similar slots. That is a resolution failure inside a segment rather than a legality failure across the space, so it is *not* the unmasked-objective story tested and dropped above — 58.8% of mass already lands on legal slots. The cheap next test is whether a masked, per-segment-reweighted objective moves `SCORE AGENDA`'s 0.31, measured here before any arena time is spent.

---

## 🏆 Phase 3: Bot Personalities, Elo & Player Progression

### 1. Bot Personalities & Playstyle Archetypes — DONE
- [x] **Biased evaluation function — DONE** (`feat/bot-personalities`, 2 September 2026). `eval::Weights` carries every tunable term of `evaluate_state` as one value, with `Default` equal to the tuned constants (which keep their doc comments and measurements), and `evaluate_state_with(state, side, registry, &Weights)` is what `HeuristicAgent`, `MctsAgent`'s leaves and rollouts, and the uniform PUCT evaluator's value head now score with; `evaluate_state` is the default-weights case and every existing caller — the gym's shaped reward included — is untouched. **Measured: heuristic-vs-heuristic (96 games, seed 1) is byte-identical to `noise-hh-seed1.json`**, so the refactor changed no balanced decision.
- [x] **Configurable bot traits — DONE** (same branch). `netrunner_bots::Personality { Balanced, Rush, Glacier, Trap, Aggressive, Cautious }`, each a `Weights` that differs from the default in a few documented places (`personality.rs`, every number a ratio against the constant it replaces). **A personality is a bias, not a plan**: it ranks the same legal candidates differently and can add no rule, card preference or script, which is what keeps one cheap to add and impossible to make illegal. Each archetype is written for one chair (`Personality::side`). Reached by `--corp-personality`/`--runner-personality` on the CLI, `--bot-personality` on the daemon (part of the bot's rating id when not balanced: `bot:heuristic:rush`), and `kind:personality` in `bench --bots` (`heuristic:rush`, participant id likewise).
  - **Two terms exist only for profiles, at zero by default:** `opponent_grip_weight` (a Corp that wants the Runner's grip thin — Trap) and `installed_agenda_weight` (a Corp that wants the agenda on the table before the ICE — Rush). *(The Trap retune below measured `opponent_grip_weight` as inert and removed it from the only profile that used it; it has no user today.)* The second was not in the first cut, and the measurement is why it exists: every unrezzed install is worth the same flat weight, so a rush profile built from advancement and protection alone had no way to say "install the agenda", and advanced *less* than balanced (`CardAdvanced` 1,045 → 1,032) because the one-ply Corp already advances every agenda it has installed.
  - **Measured, heuristic-vs-heuristic, 96 games, seed 1, each archetype against a balanced opponent** (balanced: Corp 42 wins + 7 flatlines, Runner 47; `RunInitiated` 2,775, `IceEncountered` 426, `AgendaScored` 200):
    | archetype | outcome | what moved |
    |---|---|---|
    | Corp `rush` | Corp **55** (46 + 9), Runner 41 | `CardAdvanced` 1,045 → 1,116, `IceRezzed` 975 → 1,081, `AgendaScored` 200 → 208 |
    | Corp `glacier` | Corp 47 (41 + 6), Runner 49 | `AgendaScored` 200 → 184, steps +4% — within the seed-spread band |
    | Corp `trap` | Corp **37** (33 + 4), Runner 59 | `IceRezzed` 975 → 835, flatlines 7 → 4, `AgendaStolen` 208 → 235 |
    | Runner `aggressive` | Corp 46 (**12** + 34 flatlines), Runner 50 | `RunInitiated` 2,775 → 3,125, `AgendaScored` 200 → 92, flatlines 7 → **34** |
    | Runner `cautious` | Corp 42 (42 + **0**), Runner **54** | `IceEncountered` 426 → 707, `SubroutineFired` 499 → 650, flatlines 7 → 0 |
    The Runner archetypes are unmistakable and real, and both clear the band by an order of magnitude: the aggressive Runner keeps the Corp from scoring at all and dies for it; the cautious one never flatlines and wins more than balanced. Rush clears the band on the outcome split (+13 Corp wins). Glacier is within it. **Trap does not work and is recorded as such**: the sample decks give the term nothing to pull on — the Corp cannot *deal* the damage it now values — and its other biases (keep credits, install more) only cost it rezzes; a real trap archetype needs a lever that recognises an ambush by its text (an `OnAccess` `DealDamage`, an advanceable non-agenda), which is card-inspection the evaluator does not do yet. Left open rather than tuned into a different name. *(Closed 3 September 2026 — see the ambush-lever and Trap-retune entries below. The lever exists, and once every generic knob in this profile was deleted in favour of it, Trap beats balanced on all five seeds, 460 Corp wins to 439.)*
  - **On the benchmark ladder** (12 games a pairing, seed 1): Corp column `glacier` 1575 ± 138 (30-18), `rush` 1470 ± 143 (30-18), `trap` 1535 ± 131, balanced 1404 ± 142 (23-25); Runner column `cautious` 1398 (14-22) under `aggressive` 1554 (19-17) and balanced 1585 (20-16) — cautious wins against the balanced Corp but loses to itself as Corp (10-2), which is the fort the cautious Runner builds being the fort a glacier-ish Corp scores behind. `target/coverage/bench-{corp,runner}-personalities.json`.
  - Pinned by `a_rush_corp_advances_where_a_glacier_corp_installs_ice` (the same position, two agents, two different actions), the `Weights` tests in `eval`, and the `Personality` tests (round-trip through names, each profile's direction, each profile's chair). Random-vs-random is untouched — the random bot has no evaluator.
  - Not built: a `puct-onnx` personality (its value head is the network's), and profiles for the search agents beyond the shared leaf evaluation — a personality could also shape PUCT's priors, and does not.
- [x] **A search baseline for the evaluator's two blindnesses — MEASURED, 3 September 2026** (`target/coverage/puct-baseline.json`). Before adding an `eval::Weights` term for the three cards Phase 1 §8 leaves reached only by their own tests, the question was whether they are blind spots of the *evaluator* or of the *one-ply seat*. A search agent can look past a parked decision, so `puct@32` vs `puct@32` over one pass of the 192-matchup cross product is the control. **Run as 192 single-game processes** (game `i` plays `matchups()[i]` at seed `1 + i`, seven at a time) merged by summing every counter, the way `Coverage::merge` does — the sequential `--headless` driver is ~54 minutes for this.

    | | random | heuristic | **puct@32** |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | *Measured Response* played | 0 | 0 | **1** |
    | *Off the Books* scored | 2 | 15 | 21 |
    | *Sericulture Expansion* scored | 2 | 32 | 26 |
    | Dividend triggers fired | 0 | 0 | **0** |
    | `AdvanceCard` | 500 | 1,828 | 3,270 |
    | paid choices accepted / declined | 124 / 213 | 65 / 451 | **33 / 672** |

  - **Measured Response is a one-ply blindness, and search finds it.** Its first play in any seating, `measured_response/OnPlay` fired once. A card whose entire value is what it does to the *opponent* scores `−5 × own_credit_weight` and nothing else to a seat that evaluates only the state in front of it; a search that plays the Runner's answer out sees the rest. One play in 192 games is thin, but it is the difference between unreachable and rare — and it confirms the damage/pressure term as the same missing lever the `Trap` archetype above needed.
  - **Dividends is not a horizon problem, and this measurement is why we know.** The hypothesis going in was that the payoff sits one ply past the greedy agent — accepting the discard-phase `OfferPaidChoice` parks a Corp decision worth `−unresolved_decision_weight` while the reward is the ply after. **PUCT falsifies it**: 3,270 advancements, 47 of these two agendas scored, and the trigger still fired zero times. The reason is that `PuctAgent`'s leaves evaluate with the same `evaluate_state_with`, so `eval.rs:464`'s `advancement_tokens.min(required)` zeroes the over-advanced token *at every depth* while `AGENDA_POINT_WEIGHT` pays 20 a point at every depth. **No search budget finds a move whose value is defined as zero.** So the fix is the leaf valuation of an over-advanced `dividends` agenda, not the parked-decision penalty — and any branch that changes only the penalty will measure as no change.
  - **The decline-by-reflex is real but secondary**, and search makes it worse rather than better: 33 accepts to 672 declines, against the heuristic's 65 to 451. It is a separable second term, worth its own before/after.
  - Aside, from the same run: one stall in 192 games (`Stalled/BudgetExhausted`, game 147, *Peculiarity* vs *Flow and Ebb*, exactly `MAX_STEPS`) — the only one, and the seating's known slow case rather than a deadlock, since every other game ended. `t400_memory_diamond` is the one card the PUCT seating never installed and random did (once); the report is a control, not a gate.
- [x] **`agenda_counter_weight` — the Dividends valuation, necessary and measurably not sufficient — DONE** (`feat/eval-dividends`, 3 September 2026).
